  blank, and understands F1–F12 via `fKeyBase = 0x4000003a` /
  `fKeyLast = 0x40000045`.
- The key-code constants are **repeated** here on purpose so the module stays
  dependency-free — the header warns that if a code in `keys.nim` moves, move it
  here too. They match exactly.

### 7.3 Persistence

There is **no keybind serialiser** in `keybind.nim`. `serializeWifi` is the only
serialiser and it exists to demonstrate one rule: a password is **never**
interpolated, it is replaced *structurally* by `redacted = "<redacted>"`, so no
code path can leak it.

For keybind persistence, use the SDK's own state persistence
(`sdk/core/hotstate.nim` / `state(...)`), which already serialises app state.
**CLOSED 2026-10-03 — `Chord` does round-trip, field by field, but only through hot-reload
state and only in a dev build.** The mechanism is generic, not chord-specific:

- `Chord` is a plain object of `int32` + four `bool` (`sdk/ui/keybind.nim:17-19`).
- `hotstate.nim` packs **any** object field by field: `hotstate.nim:29-30`
  `elif T is tuple or T is object: for f in fields(x): packInto(b, f)`, and unpacks it with the
  mirror `hotstate.nim:69-72`. A `seq[Binding]` nests through `hotstate.nim:21-23` / `:49-55`,
  and `ScopeKind`/`Provenance` go through the enum branch (`hotstate.nim:18` / `:42`). So
  `state[seq[Binding]](bindings)` is packed and restored whole.
- Every `state(...)` opts in — `sdk/core/signals.nim:198-203` calls `keepAcrossReloads` under
  `when not defined(release)`.

Three limits, so you do not read more into it than is there:

- **Dev builds only.** The registry and the four exports are both inside
  `when not defined(release)` (`hotstate.nim:78`, `:158`), so a release app carries no
  persistence at all.
- **It is hot reload, not disk.** The only consumer of `saveState` in the repo is the watcher
  (`render/native/engine.nim:4182`), and `host.nim:1295-1311` feeds the blob straight back into
  the next build's `run(app, restore)`. Nothing writes it to a file and reads it back on the
  next launch, so this does **not** give you "the user's keybinds survive a restart".
- **The node-prop wire is a different thing.** `Chord` has no wire ids and does not go over the
  `Node` prop protocol; `wire.nim` carries only `int32` / `float32` / `string` props.

The original claim — *nothing in the repo does that today* — still holds: a repo-wide grep for
`state[Chord]`, `state[Binding]` and `state[seq[Binding]]` returns **no matches**, and no file
under `apps/` mentions `Binding(` or imports `keypicker`. So this is a supported path, not an
exercised one.

### 7.4 Wiring a chord to a keypress

```nim
let bindings = @[Binding(id: "tiling.split", chord: Chord(code: int32(ord('b')), ctrl: true),
                         deflt: Chord(code: int32(ord('b')), ctrl: true), scopeKind: skGlobal,
                         owner: "")]

app("…"):
  onKey:
    let k = lastKey()
    let hit = Chord(code: k.code, ctrl: k.ctrl, shift: k.shift, alt: k.alt, super: false)
    for i, b in bindings:
      if sameChord(hit, b.chord) and b.scopeKind == skGlobal:
        split(); handled()
```

`super` (Cmd on macOS) is **not** exposed on `KeyPress` — `keys.nim` gives you
`shift`/`ctrl`/`alt` only, and `ctrl` is documented as "Ctrl, **or Cmd on a
Mac**". If you need a distinct Cmd chord, read `k.mods` directly against the SDL
bit (`KMOD_GUI = 0x0C00`; the engine's own masks are `0x0003` shift, `0x0CC0`
ctrl, `0x0300` alt — note these are *aggregated* masks, not single bits).

---

## 8. Drag and drop

### 8.1 What exists

```nim
proc onDragStart*(node: Node, handler: proc())
proc onDragOver*(node: Node, handler: proc())
proc onDrop*(node: Node, handler: proc())
proc onDrop*(node: Node, handler: proc(f: DroppedFile))   # a second overload

type DroppedFile* = object
  name*: string
  data*: string

proc droppedData*(): string
proc droppedName*(): string
proc droppedFile*(): DroppedFile
proc setDragData*(data: string)
```

DSL: `ondragstart:`, `ondragover:`, `ondrop:`.

### 8.2 The three event paths, verified

| Trigger | What happens |
|---|---|
| **In-app drag** | `pointerDown` on a node with a DragStart handler sets `e.draggingNode` and fires `E_DRAGSTART` (`engine.nim:2952-2955`). While dragging, every `pointerMove` fires `E_DRAGOVER` on the **first node in the hit chain** that has a DragOver handler (`engine.nim:2667-2670`, deepest-first, `break` after the first). `pointerUp` fires `E_DROP` on the first node in the chain with a Drop handler (`engine.nim:3045-3052`). |
| **OS file drop** | `Engine.dropFile(path, x, y)` (`engine.nim:3990-4013`) reads up to **16 MiB** of the file into `dropData`, sets `dropName = extractFilename(path)`, then fires Drop on the deepest node with a Drop handler (or the root). |
| **Right click** | `contextClick`, §1.2 — opens the nearest `contextmenu` node and fires `oncontextmenu:`. |

`render/native/t_dnd.nim` is the executable proof of all of this: it drives a
press on "Source", a move over "Drop Target" and a release, asserting
`dragging` → `over target` → `dropped node card-item`, then calls
`eng.dropFile(...)` and asserts `dropped file dropped_sample.txt:File drop payload 12345`.

### 8.3 The honest gap: what actually crosses

`Engine.dropFile` sets `e.s.dropData`, and the in-app path sets
`e.s.dropData = e.dragData` — where **`e.dragData` is set to `""` at drag start
and nothing in the repo ever sets it to anything else** (`engine.nim:2954`).
There is **no `y_drag_*` import** in `host.nim`.

- **OS file drops carry a real payload**: `droppedName()` and `droppedData()`.
- **In-app drops carry an empty string natively.** The tested working pattern
  (and the one `t_dnd.nim` exercises) is: **put the payload in your own `state`
  at DragStart and read it at Drop.** `setDragData(s)` only reaches
  `localDragData`, which `droppedData()` falls back to on **wasm32 builds**
  (`sdk/core/runtime.nim:203-210` — the `yDropData` call is inside
  `when sizeof(pointer) == 4`).

`apps/minipaint/PORT-NOTES.md:1849-1859` records the related web gap: on web
`y_drop_name` is stubbed and `y_drop_data`/`y_pick_*` are missing, so the
drag-and-drop *source* side is missing on the web target. `PORT-NOTES.md:335-350`
notes `y_drop_name` is stubbed on web while dropped files still have a name on
native. **Native is the only target where file drop is real.**

### 8.4 Drag recipes

```nim
## Click-drag (what you want for a canvas or a slider)
let dragging = state false
canvas @c(width = 400.px, height = 200.px, draw = Draw().cmds):
  onPointerDown: dragging.set(true); let (x, y) = pointerPos(); pos.set((x, y))
  onPointerMove:
    if dragging.get(): let (x, y) = pointerPos(); pos.set((x, y))
  onPointerUp:   dragging.set(false)
```
PointerMove keeps landing on the pressed node while it is pressed
(§1.2), so this needs no capture and no DragStart handler.

```nim
## Real drag & drop between two nodes
let payload = state ""                  # YOUR state is the payload — see §8.3
app("Dnd"):
  panel @card("Drag me"):
    onDragStart: payload.set("card-item")
  panel @zone("Drop here"):
    onDragOver: hoverZone.set(true)
    onDrop:
      if payload.get().len > 0: adopt(payload.get())
      payload.set("")

## File drop (native only)
input @f(placeholder = "or drop a file"):
  onDrop:
    let (n, d) = droppedName(), droppedData()
    if n.len > 0: readIt(n, d)
```
---

## 9. Recipe: porting an HTML page's keyboard + mouse

The two rules that make ports work:

1. **Keys go on the app root. Mouse goes where it happens.**
2. **Pointer state is your own `state`.** There are no event objects.

```nim
## Shortcuts on the root — the ancestor walk starts at the focus and climbs to 0,
## and it NEVER descends, so a canvas-level onKey is unreachable.
app("Port", width = 640, height = 480):
  onKey:
    let k = lastKey()
    if k.ctrl and k == 'z': undo(); handled()
    elif k == keyDelete: removeSelected(); handled()   # take Delete from a field
```

**Mapping table for a JS page:**

| In the page | In Yantra |
|---|---|
| `document.addEventListener('keydown')` | `onKey:` on the **app root** |
| `e.key === 'ArrowLeft'` | `lastKey() == keyLeft` (or `keyHeld(keyLeft)` in `onFrame:`) |
| `e.key === 'a'` / `e.code === 'KeyA'` | `k == 'a'` — case-folded, always lower-case ASCII |
| `e.ctrlKey \|\| e.metaKey` | `k.ctrl` |
| `e.shiftKey` / `e.altKey` | `k.shift` / `k.alt` |
| `e.preventDefault()` | `handled()` |
| `canvas.onmousedown` | `onPointerDown:` on the canvas; `pointerPos()` |
| `window.addEventListener('mousemove')` | `onPointerMove:` on the root (root sees everything) |
| `e.clientX / clientY` | `pointerPage()` (page px) |
| `canvas.getBoundingClientRect()` maths | `pointerPos()` (already node-local px) |
| `requestAnimationFrame(cb)` | `onFrame:` on the root, `frameDt()` |
| CSS `:hover { transform: scale(1.04) }` | `hover: scale = 1.04` + `transition = 200` |
| CSS `transition: all .2s` | `transition = 200` (colours, opacity, hover scale/lift only) |
| `setTimeout(fn, ms)` | `after 200.ms: …` / `afterMs(200'f32, …)` |
| `element.focus()` | no API — click it, or make it the first tab stop |
| `draggable="true"` / `ondrop` | `ondragstart:` + `ondrop:`, payload via your own `state` |
| `input.maxLength` / `pattern` | **not implemented** — enforce in `onInput` |
| `tabindex="3"` | **not implemented natively** — tab order is document order |

## 10. Recipe: a chess board (click-to-select + drag-to-move)

The shape every board / tool / puzzle port needs. Eight squares of `state`, a
selection, and a drag that moves the piece.

```nim
## 8 squares as 64 state cells; -1 empty, else the piece id.
var board = newSeq[int](64)
let sel = state -1                 # the selected square, or -1
let cursor = state -1              # what the keyboard cursor is on
let grabbed = state -1             # the square a drag took its piece from

const N = 8
proc sq(f, r: int): int = r * N + f

proc squareAt(x, y: float32): int =
  ## pointerPos() is already in this canvas's own pixels: subtract the origin and
  ## divide by the cell size. Add the canvas's own scroll offset if it scrolls.
  let f = int(x) div CELL
  let r = int(y) div CELL
  if f < 0 or f >= N or r < 0 or r >= N: -1 else: sq(f, r)

proc pieceAt(i: int): int =
  if i < 0 or i >= board.len: -1 else: board[i]

proc movePiece(from, to: int) =
  if from >= 0 and from < board.len and to >= 0 and to < board.len and board[from] != -1:
    board[to] = board[from]
    board[from] = -1
    version.set(version.get + 1)   # the bump that makes `draw` re-emit
```

**Click to select, click again to move** (the accessible baseline):

```nim
canvas @board(w = CELL * N, h = CELL * N,
              draw = drawBoard(board, sel.get, cursor.get)):
  onPointerDown:
    let i = squareAt(pointerPos())
    if i < 0: return
    if sel.get >= 0 and pieceAt(i) == -1: movePiece(sel.get, i)
    elif pieceAt(i) != -1: sel.set(i)
    else: sel.set(-1)
```

**Drag to move** — three lines more, and it needs no capture, because
`onPointerMove` keeps firing on the pressed node:

```nim
  onPointerDown:
    let i = squareAt(pointerPos())
    if i >= 0 and pieceAt(i) != -1:
      grabbed.set(i); sel.set(i)
    elif grabbed.get >= 0:
      movePiece(grabbed.get, i); grabbed.set(-1); sel.set(-1)
  onPointerMove:
    if grabbed.get >= 0:
      hoverSq.set(squareAt(pointerPos()))    # for the ghost/preview
  onPointerUp:
    grabbed.set(-1)
```

**Arrow keys** — on the **root**, with `handled()` so the app takes the arrows
rather than letting a focused field scroll:

```nim
app("Chess"):
  onKey:
    let k = lastKey()
    ## KeyPress is an object, so it cannot go in a `case` — use if/elif with the
    ## `==` overload (or switch on k.code, an int32).
    if grabbed.get >= 0: return              # a drag owns the board
    if k == keyLeft:  cursor.set(step(cursor.get, -1, 0)); handled()
    elif k == keyRight: cursor.set(step(cursor.get, +1, 0)); handled()
    elif k == keyUp:    cursor.set(step(cursor.get,  0, -1)); handled()
    elif k == keyDown:  cursor.set(step(cursor.get,  0, +1)); handled()
    elif k == keyEnter:
      if sel.get < 0 and pieceAt(cursor.get) != -1: sel.set(cursor.get)
      elif sel.get >= 0 and sel.get != cursor.get:
        movePiece(sel.get, cursor.get); sel.set(-1)
      handled()
    elif k == keyEscape: sel.set(-1); handled()
```

The four things that make this port correct, and each is a measured Yantra fact:

1. `pointerPos()` is **node-local px** — no `getBoundingClientRect()` maths.
2. The arrow/Enter handler is on the **root**, because the ancestor walk never
   descends to the canvas (`ENGINE-ISSUES.md:208-241`).
3. `handled()` stops the built-in action, so the arrows do not also scroll a
   field or the window.
4. Every state change bumps something the `draw` expression reads, which is what
   makes the canvas repaint. Chapter 10 §6 explains the loop.

---

## 11. A complete example

A canvas that responds to pointer drag and to the arrow keys. Compile-verified
with Nim 2.2.10 against `sdk/` using the headless harness from
`sdk/tests/t_style.nim` (all `y_node_*` / `y_input` / `y_node_size` /
`y_key` / `yantra_get_dt` imports stubbed): it builds 5 nodes and runs clean.

That check caught two real mistakes worth remembering:
`focused: border = …` is a `Y013` compile error, and **`KeyPress` cannot be used
in a `case`** (`selector must be of an ordinal type, float or string`) — write
`if k == keyLeft: … elif …`.

```nim
## Drag a dot around a canvas with the pointer; nudge it with the arrow keys.

const CW = 400
const CH = 200

let dotX = state 100'f32
let dotY = state 100'f32
let dragging = state false
let ticks = state 0

proc clampf(v, lo, hi: float32): float32 = (if v < lo: lo elif v > hi: hi else: v)

app("Drag + Keys", width = 460, height = 300, fill = 0x101725):
  ## Keys live on the APP ROOT: the key walk goes focus -> ancestors -> root and
  ## never descends, so a canvas-level onKey would never be reached.
  onKey:
    let k = lastKey()
    if k == keyLeft:  dotX.set(clampf(dotX.get - 12, 0, CW - 1)); handled()
    elif k == keyRight: dotX.set(clampf(dotX.get + 12, 0, CW - 1)); handled()
    elif k == keyUp:    dotY.set(clampf(dotY.get - 12, 0, CH - 1)); handled()
    elif k == keyDown:  dotY.set(clampf(dotY.get + 12, 0, CH - 1)); handled()
    elif k == keyEscape: dragging.set(false); handled()
    else: discard

  column(gap = 10.px, pad = 16.px):

    text @hud("{int(dotX.get)}, {int(dotY.get)} · moves {ticks.get}",
          size = 14, color = 0x8CA0C0)

    ## `draw` is re-read every paint. Because the expression reads state, the
    ## effect re-runs whenever that state changes and the canvas repaints.
    canvas @field(width = CW.px, height = CH.px,
                  draw = Draw()
                            .rect(0, 0, CW, CH, 0xFF162033'u32)
                            .line(0, dotY.get, CW, dotY.get, 0xFF24354Fu32, 1'f32)
                            .line(dotX.get, 0, dotX.get, CH, 0xFF24354Fu32, 1'f32)
                            .circle(dotX.get, dotY.get, 10, 0xFFFFB86B'u32)
                            .cmds):

      ## pointerPos() is in THIS canvas's own pixels — no rect maths needed.
      onPointerDown:
        let (x, y) = pointerPos()
        dragging.set(true)
        dotX.set(clampf(x, 0, CW - 1)); dotY.set(clampf(y, 0, CH - 1))
        ticks.set(ticks.get + 1)

      ## PointerMove keeps arriving here while the node is pressed, so this is
      ## the whole drag loop — no capture, no DragStart.
      onPointerMove:
        if dragging.get:
          let (x, y) = pointerPos()
          dotX.set(clampf(x, 0, CW - 1)); dotY.set(clampf(y, 0, CH - 1))
          ticks.set(ticks.get + 1)

      onPointerUp:
        dragging.set(false)

    button @reset("Reset", fill = 0x24354F, radius = 8.px):
      onClick:
        dotX.set(100); dotY.set(100); dragging.set(false)
```

### Verified limits checklist for a port

- `pointerPos()` is node-local px, `pointerPage()` is page px, and both are only
  fresh **inside** a pointer handler.
- Layout transforms (`scale`, `rotate`) are **not** applied to pointer
  coordinates; do the inverse transform yourself.
- A canvas that scrolls its own content (`pCanvasWidth` / `pCanvasHeight`, set
  imperatively) needs its scroll offset added by hand (`yNodeSize(id, 2)` for y,
  `yNodeSize(id, 9)` for x).
- Key handlers on a child node are unreachable — root only.
- No `keyUp` event: use `keyHeld` in `onFrame:`, or debounce `onKey:` yourself,
  because auto-repeat re-fires `onKey:` indistinguishably.
- No `tabIndex`, no `maxLength`, no `pattern`, no `element.focus()` natively.
- In-app drag payloads are empty natively: use your own `state`.
- `pointerIsLocked()` is always false — track your own lock state.


================================================================
FILE: skills/web-to-yantra/DOM-EVENTS-TO-SDL3.md
================================================================
# DOM events → SDL3 events

`keydown`, `click`, `pointerdown`, `requestAnimationFrame`, `setTimeout`,
`localStorage`, `element.animate`, `visibilitychange`, pointer capture, and — the hard
one — **hit-testing a canvas you drew yourself**.

**Sources:** `sdk/ui/keys.nim` (48 lines, read in full), `sdk/ui/input.nim` (65 lines),
`sdk/core/runtime.nim:132-193` (`set`, `addHandler`, `onClick`), `sdk/core/wire.nim:322-356`
(the `Event` enum), `sdk/core/persist.nim:34` (`persistent`), `sdk/ui/motion.nim:74-99`
(`afterMs`, `after`, `timeline`), `sdk/yantra.nim:437-467` (`animate`, `transition`),
`host/host.nim:589-623` + `render/native/engine.nim:3016-3023` (`pointerPos`),
`sdk/ui/sheet.nim:140-175` (a real drag implementation), `apps/typewriter/app.nim`
(a working port of a keyboard-driven page).

## 0. The whole map

The wire has 34 events (`wire.nim:322-356`). The ones a port needs:

| DOM | Yantra | Event id | Read the event with |
|---|---|---|---|
| `keydown` | `evKey` | 15 | `lastKey()` → `KeyPress`, then `handled()` |
| `click` | `evClick` | 0 | `pointerPos()` (canvas-local px) |
| `pointerdown` / `mousemove` / `pointerup` | `evPointerDown` / `evPointerMove` / `evPointerUp` | 20 / 21 / 22 | `pointerPos()` |
| `requestAnimationFrame` | `evFrame` | 33 | `frameDt()` → **seconds** |
| `setTimeout` / `setInterval` | — | — | `afterMs(ms, body)` / a frame counter |
| `<input>` `input` / `change` | `evInput` / `evChange` | 3 / 1 | the bound `Signal` |
| `scroll` | `evScroll` | 6 | `yNodeSize(id, 2)` (y) and `, 9` (x) |
| `resize` (element) | `evResize` | 12 | `yNodeSize(id, 0/1)` |
| `dragstart` / `dragover` / `drop` | `evDragStart` / `evDragOver` / `evDrop` | 30 / 31 / 32 | `droppedFile()` |
| `focus` / `blur` | `evFocus` / `evBlur` | 4 / 5 | |
| `element.animate()` | — | — | `node.animate(…)` for nodes; a `var` + `evFrame` for canvas |
| `visibilitychange` | **none** | — | see §8 |
| `setPointerCapture` | **none** | — | see §9 |
| `window.resize` | **none** | — | see §10 |

Two structural differences to internalise before reading on:

- **A handler is a bare `proc()` with no arguments.** Everything the event carries is
  read from a *global-ish* getter: `lastKey()`, `pointerPos()`. There is no event
  object, no `event.target`, no `stopPropagation`.
- **Handlers are per (node, event) and run innermost-first**, and `handled()` stops the
  key going further — no outer handler and no built-in action
  (`keys.nim:1-4`). That is the whole propagation model.

```nim
proc addHandler*(node: Node; event: Event; handler: proc()) =     # runtime.nim:174-185
  ## Any number of handlers per node and event, run in the order they were added
  ## (an `input` bound to a signal can also have its own `onInput:`). The engine
  ## sees one handler per (node, event): a dispatcher over this list.
```

---

## 1. `keydown` → `evKey` + `lastKey()`

```nim
type KeyPress* = object
  code*: int32     ## SDL key code: a letter or digit is its lower-case ASCII code
  mods*: int32     ## modifier bits

proc lastKey*(): KeyPress = KeyPress(code: yKey(0), mods: yKey(1))   # keys.nim:41
proc handled*() = yKeyHandled()                                     # keys.nim:42
proc shift*(k: KeyPress): bool = (k.mods and 0x0003) != 0           # keys.nim:43
proc ctrl*(k: KeyPress): bool = (k.mods and 0x0CC0) != 0            # keys.nim:44 — Ctrl, or Cmd on a Mac
proc alt*(k: KeyPress): bool = (k.mods and 0x0300) != 0             # keys.nim:45
proc `==`*(k: KeyPress; code: int32): bool = k.code == code         # keys.nim:46
proc `==`*(k: KeyPress; c: char): bool =                           # keys.nim:47-48
  k.code == int32(ord(if c in {'A'..'Z'}: char(ord(c) + 32) else: c))
```

The named non-ASCII keys are consts (`keys.nim:18-39`): `keyBackspace = 8`,
`keyTab = 9`, `keyEnter = 13`, `keyEscape = 27`, `keySpace = 32`, `keyDelete = 127`,
`keyLeft/Right/Up/Down`, `keyHome`, `keyEnd`, `keyPageUp/Down`, `keyPeriod`,
`keyComma`, plus `keyShift`/`keyCtrl`/`keyAlt` in `input.nim:13-15` for `keyHeld`.

**`k == 'a'` folds case** (`keys.nim:47-48`), so one comparison covers `A` and `a` — but
only for letters. Digits and punctuation have no such fold: `k == 'A'` and `k == 'a'`
are the same test, `k == '1'` is only `'1'`.

The port's whole keyboard handler, which is also a good model for the shape of the
answer (`app.nim:1019-1028`):

```nim
page.addHandler(evKey, proc() =
  let k = lastKey()
  if k.ctrl or k.alt: return                     # leave shortcuts to the host
  if k == keyEnter: carriageReturn(); handled(); return
  if k == keyBackspace: backspace(); handled(); return
  if k == keyTab: doTab(); handled(); return
  if k == keySpace: doSpace(); handled(); return
  if k.code >= 32 and k.code < 127:
    typeChar(char(k.code))
    handled())
```

Four decisions in that snippet, all worth copying:

1. **Register on `page` (the window root), not on a control.** `keys.nim:1-3`: the root's
   handler runs for every key. Registering on a button means the key only arrives when
   that button has focus — the single most common port mistake.
2. **`if k.ctrl or k.alt: return` before anything else**, so Ctrl-C is never eaten.
   Without it, a text field's copy shortcut dies.
3. **The printable range is `32 ..< 127`.** Everything else is a named const. Note this
   delivers *unshifted* ASCII: `k.code` is the lower-case letter's code, so `A` arrives
   as 65 and shift state is only in `k.shift(k)`. The port derives the uppercase form
   itself (`app.nim:900: if ch in {'a'..'z'} and up: ch = char(ord(ch) - 32)`).
4. **`handled()` on every branch that consumed the key.** It is what stops the engine's
   own behaviour (a focused field's text entry, a button's Enter) from also firing.

`shift` on `KeyPress` is a proc, so it reads `k.shift` — and it is both shift keys
(`0x0003`). There is **no `keypress` vs `keydown` distinction**: a key press that
auto-repeats arrives repeatedly, so a handler that advances state must be idempotent or
rate-limited itself.

For a *game* loop you want the other input API entirely — polling, not events
(`input.nim:33-36`): `keyHeld('w')`, `keyHeld(keyLeft)`, `mouseHeld(1)`,
`mouseDelta()`, `frameDt()`.

---

## 2. `click` / `pointerdown` → `evClick` + `pointerPos()`

`pointerPos()` returns **the pointer in the canvas's own pixels**, not window
coordinates (`input.nim:54-56`; computed in the engine at `engine.nim:3016-3023` as
`(x - b.x, y + scrollY - b.y)`):

```nim
proc pointerPos*(): (float32, float32) = (yNodeSize(0, 7), yNodeSize(0, 8))
  ## In onPointerDown / onPointerMove / onPointerUp on a canvas: where the pointer is, in the
  ## canvas's own pixels (painting, dragging on a canvas).
```

`pointerPage()` (`input.nim:57-58`, axes 13/14) is the same point in window space, when
you need it.

The typewriter's handler is the whole pattern (`app.nim:1014-1017`):

```nim
scene.onClick(proc() =
  let (px, py) = pointerPos()
  let k = hitKey(px, py)
  if k.len > 0: onScreenKey(k))
```

Note: **no coordinates are passed to the handler**; you read them, and you read them
once, at the top.

---

## 3. Hit-testing your own drawn keys — the real problem

A `button` node hit-tests itself. A canvas does not, so *you* write the geometry test,
and you must write it with the **same numbers the drawing used**. The single most
important rule: **the hit-test and the draw must call the same layout function.**

The drawing used these (`app.nim:513-519`):

```nim
const ROWS = [
  (y: 150.0'f32, r: 16.5'f32, sp: 58.0'f32, dx: 10.0'f32, n: 12),
  (y: 190.0'f32, r: 17.5'f32, sp: 61.0'f32, dx:  6.0'f32, n: 11),
  (y: 230.0'f32, r: 18.5'f32, sp: 64.0'f32, dx:  3.0'f32, n: 11),
  (y: 270.0'f32, r: 19.5'f32, sp: 67.0'f32, dx:  0.0'f32, n: 12)
]
const ROWKEYS = ["1234567890-BS", "TABqwertyuiop", "LOCKasdfghjkl;", "SHIFTzxcvbnm,./SHIFT"]
```

`drawKeys()` computes each key's position from `ROWS`/`ROWKEYS` (`app.nim:524-537`):

```nim
for ri in 0 .. 3:
  let row = ROWS[ri]
  let keys = ROWKEYS[ri]
  let n = row.n
  let x0 = 500.0'f32 + row.dx - (n - 1).float32 * row.sp / 2.0'f32
  for i in 0 ..< n:
    let k = $keys[i]
    let x = cx(x0 + i.float32 * row.sp)
    let y = by(row.y)
```

and `hitKey()` recomputes **the identical expression**, then inflates the test box by a
few pixels so small targets stay tappable (`app.nim:857-880`):

```nim
proc hitKey(px, py: float32): string =
  ## Which illustrated key did the pointer land on? Canvas space: subtract HEAD.
  let cyv = py + HEAD
  if cyv < by(140.0): return ""
  # space bar
  if px > cx(292) and px < cx(708) and cyv > by(284) and cyv < by(310):
    return " "
  for ri in 0 .. 3:
    let row = ROWS[ri]
    let keys = ROWKEYS[ri]
    let n = row.n
    let x0 = 500.0'f32 + row.dx - (n - 1).float32 * row.sp / 2.0'f32
    for i in 0 ..< n:
      let kx = cx(x0 + i.float32 * row.sp)
      let ky = by(row.y)
      if px > kx - row.r * U - 4 and px < kx + row.r * U + 4 and
         cyv > ky - row.r * 0.8'f32 * U and cyv < ky + row.r * 0.8'f32 * U + 6:
        return $keys[i]
  # the carriage-return lever, which rides with the carriage
  let mid = SW / 2.0'f32 - (PW / 2.0'f32) * S
  let lx = cx(900.0) + (carX - mid)
  if px > lx and px < lx + 100.0'f32 * U and cyv > cy(-8.0) and cyv < cy(54.0):
    return "@RETURN"
  ""
```

Every line of that is a decision:

- **`let cyv = py + HEAD`** converts canvas-local back to the scene coordinates the art
  was authored in (`app.nim:858`). The canvas node sits below a 64 px header
  (`app.nim:952, 1010`), so window-space y = canvas y + header height. Get this wrong
  and every hit test is offset by exactly the header height — the classic symptom is
  "clicking the bottom row types into the top row".
- **The `cx()`/`by()` helpers, not raw numbers.** The drawing goes through
  `proc cx(x: float32): float32 = MX + x * U` and `proc by(y: float32): float32 = MY + 100.0'f32 * U + y * U`
  (`app.nim:377-379`), so the hit-test goes through the same two procs. If the window
  size or the scale ever changes, one edit fixes both.
- **The test box is inflated**: `-4 … +4` in x and `+6` at the bottom. The drawn cap is
  an ellipse (`ry = r * 0.8`), but a rectangular test box is right for a keyboard — and
  a few pixels of slack is the difference between a key you can hit and one you cannot.
- **Return a *label*, not an index**, and dispatch on it afterwards (`onScreenKey`,
  `app.nim:882-902`). Keys that are words (`"BS"`, `"TAB"`, `"SHIFT"`, `"LOCK"`) and
  keys that are characters then share one hit-test.
- **Moving targets are hit-tested at their current position.** The carriage-return lever
  rides the carriage: `lx = cx(900.0) + (carX - mid)` (`app.nim:877`) uses the live
  `carX`, not a stored rect. Same for the bell marker, which the port keeps behind a
  forward-declared helper for exactly this reason
  (`proc midBell(): float32` at `app.nim:572`, defined at `:591`, used by both the draw
  and the hit paths).

**Alternative worth knowing:** if the drawn thing is a grid of items, reuse the SDK's
own grid hit-test. `sdk/ui/sheet.nim:160` does `let (r, c) = s.hit(x, y)` after
`let x = yNodeSize(id, 7)` / `let y = yNodeSize(id, 8)` — the same read, the same
"return −1 / a sentinel when nothing was hit" contract.

**When to skip all of this:** if the widget is a discrete control, make it a **node**
laid over the canvas. The typewriter does exactly that for its header (`app.nim:951-987`)
and gets focus, hover, pressed states and hit-testing for free. Drawing a button *and*
hand-writing its hit-test is only worth it when the button is part of an illustration.

---

## 4. `requestAnimationFrame(loop)` → `evFrame` + `frameDt()`

```nim
proc frameDt*(): float32 = yInput(7, 0)
  ## Seconds since the previous frame: move things by speed * frameDt().    # input.nim:45-46
```

**`frameDt()` is in seconds.** The page's `t` in milliseconds becomes
`nowMs += frameDt() * 1000.0'f32` (`app.nim:906`). This is the single most common unit
bug in a port: a 60 ms spring multiplied straight by `frameDt()` moves 60× too little.

```nim
scene.addHandler(evFrame, proc() = tick())     # app.nim:1030
```

Register the frame handler on the canvas you are repainting — or on the window, if
several things animate. The port's whole animation loop (`app.nim:905-922`):

```nim
proc tick() =
  nowMs += frameDt() * 1000.0'f32
  # escapement snap on a keystroke, slower overshooting glide on return
  let k = min(1.0'f32, frameDt() * 9.0)
  carX = carX + (carTarget - carX) * k
  paperY = paperY + (paperTarget - paperY) * min(1.0'f32, frameDt() * 11.0)
  strikeFlash = max(0.0'f32, strikeFlash - frameDt() * 4.0)
  bellFlash = max(0.0'f32, bellFlash - frameDt() * 1.4)
  if dingT >= 0:
    dingT += frameDt() * 1000.0'f32
    if dingT > 1400.0: dingT = -1.0
  if toastMsg.len > 0:
    toastT += frameDt()
    if toastT > 2.6:
      toastMsg = ""
  if litKey.len > 0:
    litKeyTimer += frameDt()
    if litKeyTimer > 0.11: litKey = ""
```

What that shows:

- **Frame-rate-independent easing** is `v += (target - v) * min(1, dt * rate)`. The `k`
  factor is computed from `frameDt()` so the same animation runs at 30 fps and 144 fps.
  This is the `ease`-by-hand version of what `node.animate` does for nodes (§7).
- **Every transient is a countdown in seconds**: `strikeFlash - dt * 4.0` is a 250 ms
  flash; `toastT > 2.6` is a 2.6 s toast; `litKeyTimer > 0.11` is the source's 110 ms
  key flash. Negative sentinel = "hidden" (`dingT = -1.0`, `app.nim:106`).
- **The frame handler does not repaint for you.** `tick()` mutates state; the repaint
  happens in whatever triggered it, or you add `repaint()` at the end of `tick()` if the
  animation is the only thing moving.

A caveat, now resolved from source: **`frameDt()` does NOT return 0 on the first
frame.** `render/native/engine.nim:3241` computes it as

```nim
e.s.frameDt = if e.lastFrameAt > 0: float32((e.now - e.lastFrameAt) / 1000) else: 1'f32 / 60
```

so the first frame is a synthetic **1/60 s**, and every later frame is the real
elapsed wall-clock delta. Dividing by `frameDt()` on frame one is therefore safe
by accident — but do not design around it; clamp instead (see §8).

> **CORRECTED 2026-10-03.** This section used to say *"a hidden or unfocused
> window stops producing frames, so `frameDt()` goes to 0 (or very large)"* and
> carried an unresolved verification marker because the two branches were never
> separated. They are now separated from source: `render/native/engine.nim:3241`
> shows the first frame is `1'f32/60` and every later frame is a **measured
> elapsed delta**, so a stall produces **one large value on return, not 0**. The
> marker is resolved and removed.

---

## 5. `setTimeout` / `setInterval`

**For a one-shot delay there is a real API** (`sdk/ui/motion.nim:74-81`):

```nim
proc afterMs*(ms: float32; body: proc()) =
  ## Runs `body` once, `ms` from now on the engine's clock (a real clock in a window,
  ## exact frame times when a film is rendered). Batched like an event handler.
  yAfter(int32(max(ms, 0) + 0.5), onHandler(proc() = batch(body)))

template after*(delay: untyped; body: untyped) =
  ## `after 1.2.s: step.set(2)` — a scene moves on by itself; a film is steps in time.
  afterMs(durationOf(delay), proc() = body)
```

and `timeline:` for a scripted sequence (`motion.nim:83-99`) using `at <time>:` /
`then <duration>:` steps.

**But inside a canvas, prefer a frame counter.** The port's toast and key flash are both
`setTimeout`s in the page and both are counters in `tick()` (§4). Why: `afterMs` runs on
the *engine's* clock and is batched like an event, while canvas animation is driven by
your `evFrame` handler; mixing the two gives you two clocks and a class of bugs where
something animates while everything else is paused. One counter, one clock.

The same reason applies to `setInterval`: it becomes "a countdown in the frame loop".

---

## 6. `localStorage` → `persistent[T]`

```nim
proc persistent*[T](initial: T; key: string): Signal[T] =      # persist.nim:34-49
  ## A signal holding `initial` the first time, and whatever it last held ever after.
  ## Every change is saved at once. A saved value that no longer fits the type (the app
  ## changed) is ignored.
```

It returns a `Signal[T]`, so it is a `state` that also persists: `.get()` to read,
`.set(v)` to write (saving on every change), `+=` / `-=` are overloaded for signals
(`sdk/core/signals.nim:239-243`). Storage lives in the host, in the app's own folder,
1 MB a value / 16 MB an app (`persist.nim:1-3`). **There is no filesystem access**, so
this is the only persistence there is.

Three typed keys in the port (`app.nim:693-694`):

```nim
var saved      = persistent("",  "marlowe44.letter.v1")
var savedMuted = persistent(0'i32, "marlowe44.muted")
```

- **Strings** for structured data, serialised by hand (`app.nim:709-714`):

```nim
proc persistNow() =
  var buf = ""
  for st in strokes:
    buf.add $st.ch & "," & $st.col & "," & $st.line & "," & $int(st.red) & ";"
  buf.add "|" & $sheetNo & "," & $col & "," & $line & "," & $int(red) & "," & $int(bellRung)
  saved.set(buf)
```

- **A plain `int32`** for a flag: `muted = savedMuted.get() == 1` (`app.nim:935`) and
  `savedMuted.set(if muted: 1'i32 else: 0'i32)` (`app.nim:982`). Use the *typed* API
  whenever the value is a scalar — the packing is free and the type check on restore is
  free too.
- **Restore is defensive parsing**, because a save file can be from an older build
  (`app.nim:833-854`): split on `|`, check `parts.len < 2`, skip short records with
  `if e.len < 3: continue`, and parse with `int32(parseInt(f[1]))` from
  `import std/strutils` (`app.nim:16`).

Note what is **not** saved: the per-stroke PRNG seeds are regenerated on restore
(`app.nim:846: seed: int32(strokes.len) * 7919 + 7919`). Because the seeds are a pure
function of the stroke index, a restored letter re-stamps identically — the same
argument as `HTML5-CANVAS-TO-SKIA.md` §8.

`localStorage` semantics you lose: there is no `localStorage.clear()` for one key, no
enumeration of keys, and no storage event across windows.

---

## 7. `element.animate()` and CSS `@keyframes`

### On a node — a real prop

```nim
proc animate*(node: Node; target: string; to: float32; duration: int32 = 300;
              easing: Easing = easeOutCubic; startVal: Option[float32] = none(float32);
              delay: int32 = 0; loop: int32 = 1; yoyo: bool = false;
              trigger: string = "mount")                             # yantra.nim:437-440
  ## target: property to animate ("opacity", "x", "y", "width", "height", "scale", "rotation")

proc transition*(node: Node; kind: string; duration: int32 = 300; easing: Easing = easeOutCubic) =
  ## kind: "enter" | "exit" | "move" | "resize" | "opacity"           # yantra.nim:462-464
```

plus the presets in `sdk/ui/motion.nim:29-48` — `fade()`, `fly()`, `pop()`, `turn()` for
enter/exit, and `spin()`, `pulse()`, `breathe()`, `bob()` for loops — usable directly as
props: `animate = spin()`, `enter = fly(y = 12)`.

The engine plays these on **its own clock**: "the app does no work per frame and nothing
re-renders" (`motion.nim:1-2`). That is the whole difference from `element.animate`:
there is no per-frame app code at all.

### In a canvas — a `var` and the frame loop

There is no `animate` for a `Draw`. Two mechanisms, and the port uses both:

1. **Hand-rolled exponential easing** in `tick()` (§4): `v = v + (target - v) * min(1, dt * rate)`.
   Right for anything that has a target value (carriage position, paper offset).
2. **A countdown with a normalised `f`** for anything time-bound — the port's `ding`
   flourish (`app.nim:679-683`):

```nim
if dingT >= 0:
  let f = dingT / 1.4'f32                       # 0 → 1 over 1.4 s
  let dx = cx((X0 - MX) / U) + 40.0'f32 * U
  d = d.text(dx, MY + 20.0'f32 * U - f * 34.0'f32,
         16.0'f32 * U, withA(cLamp, (1.0'f32 - f) * 0.9), "ding", 400)
```

`f` is then used for position, alpha, scale — one clock, any number of channels. The
toast does the same with a fade in *and* out from one counter
(`app.nim:685: let a = min(1.0'f32, min(toastT, 2.6'f32 - toastT) * 4.0'f32)`).

Keep a `Ease` function if the page used one: the page's easing curve becomes a small
`proc ease(t: float32): float32` called from `tick()` with `f`.

---

## 8. `visibilitychange`, `pagehide`, focus loss

**No event exists for this.** Grep the wire `Event` enum (`wire.nim:322-356`): there is
no visibility, no blur-window, no pagehide. What exists is `evFocus` / `evBlur`
(4 / 5) for *node* focus, and `evSystemTheme` (26).

The workable substitute is `frameDt()` itself: a hidden or un-focused window stops
producing frames, so on the first frame back your clock **jumps by the whole
stall**. Two consequences to design for:

- **Clamp the step**, or the first frame after a stall teleports everything:
  `let k = min(1.0'f32, frameDt() * 9.0)` (`app.nim:908`) bounds the easing factor at
  1 whatever `dt` was.
- **Do not run gameplay logic off wall-clock time** — use accumulated `dt`, as `tick()`
  does.

> **RESOLVED 2026-10-03 — this replaces an open verification marker.** The old text
> said it was unknown whether `frameDt()` goes to **0** or to **one large value**
> while a window is unfocused, and told you to go test it. That is settled from
> source: `render/native/engine.nim:3241` is
> `e.s.frameDt = if e.lastFrameAt > 0: float32((e.now - e.lastFrameAt) / 1000) else: 1'f32 / 60`.
> A stall produces **one large value** on return; only the synthetic first frame is
> `1/60`. So **clamp** — and now you know why, and that "goes to 0" was never the
> failure mode to fear. Host side: `host/host.nim:926` (`of 7: host.frameDt`),
> reached from `y_input(7, 0)`.

**Audio is the other thing that should stop.** The port gates every sound on the user
toggle rather than on focus (`proc playS(s: Sound) = if not muted and s.id != 0: play(s)`,
`app.nim:706-707`).

---

## 9. Pointer capture and drag

`setPointerCapture` does not exist. The pattern that replaces it is **a capture id in a
`var`**: set it on `evPointerDown`, act on it in `evPointerMove`, clear it in
`evPointerUp`. The SDK's own table implementation is 20 lines and is the reference
(`sdk/ui/sheet.nim:148-175`):

```nim
s.node.addHandler(evPointerDown, proc() =
  let x = yNodeSize(id, 7)
  let y = yNodeSize(id, 8)
  …                                     # find what was grabbed
  if r < 0: return
  s.dragCol = c                          # THE CAPTURE: which thing owns the pointer
  s.dragFrom = x
  s.dragW = w
  return)
s.node.addHandler(evPointerMove, proc() =
  if s.dragCol < 0: return               # not dragging: ignore move entirely
  s.colW[s.dragCol] = max(24'f32, s.dragW + yNodeSize(id, 7) - s.dragFrom)
  s.layoutCols()
  s.redraw())
s.node.addHandler(evPointerUp, proc() = s.dragCol = -1)
```

The invariants:

- The sentinel is `-1`, never `0` — node id 0 is a real node.
- The `if s.dragCol < 0: return` guard goes **first** in the move handler.
- A `evPointerUp` that never sees a down must be harmless; make the up handler
  unconditionally release.
- Add `if r < 0: return` on down so a miss does not arm the capture.

For a canvas drag, use `pointerPos()` in each handler and keep the last position in a
`var`. There is no `pointerId` and no `buttons` bitmask in the event model — `mouseHeld(1)`
(`input.nim:39`) is a polled check if you need it.

---

## 10. `window.resize`, media queries, reflow

**There is no reflow.** A Yantra window is the size you declared and it does not change
under you. The port says so in its own constants (`app.nim:30-32`):

```nim
SW   = 1280.0'f32    # scene width  (window is fixed: Yantra has no reflow)
HEAD  = 64.0'f32     # header strip height
SH   = 816.0'f32     # scene height
```

and drops the page's mobile branch entirely (`app.nim:347-349`): "Yantra windows are
fixed, so SW/SH are constants and K (the mobile split-travel factor) is always 1."

`evResize` (12) exists and is real — it fires when a node's laid-out size changes, and
you read it with `yNodeSize(id, 0)` / `, 1` (`sdk/yantra.nim:401-406`, `trackSize`;
`sheet.nim:140-143`). That is enough for a scroll area or a split pane to learn its own
size. It is **not** a viewport media query.

The CSS media-query equivalent is a size-driven branch, which the SDK expresses with a
`sized w, h:` block (`reference/15a` §0). For a canvas whose art depends on size, the
port's answer is: pick one size, and compute layout from constants at startup
(`proc layout()` — `app.nim:347-357`), not on every frame.

---

## 11. The port checklist for events

1. **Keys on the window root**, `handled()` on every consumed branch, ctrl/alt let
   through first, printable range `32..<127` with shift applied by you.
2. **One frame loop**, `dt` in seconds, every transient a countdown, every easing factor
   clamped to 1.
3. **One clock.** Prefer frame counters to `afterMs` for canvas animation; use
   `afterMs` / `after` / `timeline` for scripted steps between user actions.
4. **Hit-test with the same functions the draw used.** Same `cx()`/`by()`, same consts,
   same current positions for anything that moves. Inflate the box. Convert coordinates
   back to the space the art was authored in.
5. **Prefer a node** for anything that must be clicked, focused or tabbed to. Only hand-
   write hit-tests for things that are genuinely part of the illustration.
6. **Persistence**: `persistent[T]` per key; typed for scalars, hand-serialised for
   structures; parse defensively on restore.
7. **Drag**: a capture `var` set on down, checked first in move, cleared on up, with a
   `-1` sentinel.
8. **Clamp `dt`** — the first frame after a stall is the one that breaks every port.
9. **Nothing in the `Event` enum covers the page lifecycle.** Visibility, unload and
   cross-window events do not exist; design so that losing all of them is survivable.

================================================================
FILE: skills/web-to-yantra/HTML5-CANVAS-TO-SKIA.md
================================================================
# Canvas2D → Skia `Draw`, command by command

Porting a `<canvas>` means rewriting its drawing calls as `Draw` builder calls. This
chapter is the lookup table, and every code line is either quoted from the working
port `apps/typewriter/app.nim` or taken directly from `sdk/ui/paint.nim`.

**Sources for this chapter**

| Source | What it fixes |
|---|---|
| `sdk/ui/paint.nim` (124 lines, read in full) | every `Draw` proc, its exact arity, the `Blend` and `Filter` enums, `eraser`, and the `Layer` API |
| `apps/typewriter/app.nim` | the ported program itself — 1033 lines that build clean |
| `apps/typewriter/029-typewriter-letters.html` | the source page the port came from |
| `apps/minipaint/ENGINE-ISSUES.md:582-586`, `reference/16` §3.2-3.4 | the `math.round` trap and the `float64` trap |

## 0. The whole API, in the order you will need it

`Draw` is one object with a single exported field, `cmds: string` (`paint.nim:11-12`).
Every proc below returns a **new** `Draw`; nothing mutates in place. The command
letters are what ends up in `cmds` (the format the Skia shim parses, `paint.nim:12`).

| Draw proc | Command | Signature, verbatim from `paint.nim` |
|---|---|---|
| `moveTo` | `M` | `(d: Draw; x, y: float32): Draw` — `:28` |
| `lineTo` | `L` | `(d: Draw; x, y: float32): Draw` — `:29` |
| `quadTo` | `Q` | `(d: Draw; cx, cy, x, y: float32): Draw` — `:30` — "Corner arc, matching canvas `quadraticCurveTo`" |
| `curveTo` | `C` | `(d: Draw; cx1, cy1, cx2, cy2, x, y: float32): Draw` — `:35` — "matching canvas `bezierCurveTo`" |
| `arcTo` | `A` | `(d: Draw; cx, cy, radius, startDeg, sweepDeg: float32): Draw` — `:40` — "matching canvas `arc()`. Angles in degrees, clockwise" |
| `close` | `Z` | `(d: Draw): Draw` — `:45` |
| `svgPath` | `V` | `(d: Draw; path: string; x, y, scale: float32; argb: uint32; strokeWidth = 0'f32): Draw` — `:46` |
| `fill` | `F` | `(d: Draw; argb: uint32): Draw` — `:53` |
| `stroke` | `S` | `(d: Draw; argb: uint32; width: float32): Draw` — `:55` — "round joins and caps: a brush" |
| `rect` | `R` | `(d: Draw; x, y, w, h: float32; argb: uint32; radius = 0'f32): Draw` — `:57` |
| `circle` | `E` | `(d: Draw; cx, cy, r: float32; argb: uint32): Draw` — `:59` |
| `line` | `G` | `(d: Draw; x1, y1, x2, y2: float32; argb: uint32; width = 1'f32): Draw` — `:61` |
| `text` | `T` | `(d: Draw; x, y, size: float32; argb: uint32; s: string; weight = 400): Draw` — `:63` |
| `image` | `I` | `(d: Draw; x, y, w, h: float32; file: string): Draw` — `:65` |
| `layer` | `Y` | `(d: Draw; l: Layer; x, y: float32; w = 0'f32; h = 0'f32): Draw` — `:67` |
| `pixels` | `X` | `(d: Draw; buf: int32; pw, ph: int; x, y: float32; w = 0'f32; h = 0'f32): Draw` — `:70` |
| `blend` | `b` | `(d: Draw; mode: Blend): Draw` — `:73` |
| `save` / `restore` | `P` / `p` | `(d: Draw): Draw` — `:75-76` |
| `translate` / `scale` / `rotate` | `t` / `s` / `o` | `(d: Draw; …: float32): Draw` — `:77-79` |
| `alpha` | `O` | `(d: Draw; a: float32): Draw` — `:80` |
| `clip` | `K` | `(d: Draw; x, y, w, h: float32): Draw` — `:81` |

Floats are serialised to **two decimals by hand** (`paint.nim:23-26`) because the
sandbox C library cannot format them. So sub-0.01-pixel precision does not survive the
round trip — do not build anything that depends on it.

### Setting up a canvas and repainting it

```nim
var scene: Node                        # the canvas, so handlers can repaint    app.nim:691
var sceneReady = false                 #                                          app.nim:692

proc repaint() =
  if sceneReady:
    scene.set(pDraw, buildScene())     # app.nim:698-700

# in main(), after the canvas exists:
scene = canvas(page, int32(SW), int32(SH - HEAD))   # app.nim:1010
sceneReady = true                                    # app.nim:1011
scene.set(pDraw, buildScene())                       # app.nim:1012
```

Two things worth stealing:

- The `sceneReady` flag (`app.nim:692, 1011`). `repaint()` is called from key and click
  handlers; a handler registered before the canvas exists would otherwise write to a
  node id of `-1`.
- `pDraw` takes the string itself — there is no `d.cmds` accessor call to forget:
  `proc set*(node: Node; prop: Prop; value: string)` (`sdk/core/runtime.nim:138`).

## 1. Paths and shapes

```nim
# ctx.fillRect(x, y, w, h)
d = d.rect(0, 0, w, h, cBody)                                    # app.nim:127

# ctx.strokeRect(x, y, w, h) with a uniform radius
# -> two commands: fill, then stroke the same path
d = d.svgPath(P_SHELL, cx(0), by(0), U, cEnamel)
d = d.svgPath(P_SHELL, cx(0), by(0), U, cRed, 2.0'f32 * U)

# ctx.beginPath / moveTo / lineTo / stroke
d = d.save().moveTo(x, y)
     .quadTo(x + cos(a + 0.6'f32) * l * 0.5'f32, y + sin(a + 0.6'f32) * l * 0.5'f32,
             x + cos(a) * l, y + sin(a) * l)
     .stroke(c, lw).restore()                                    # app.nim:267-270

# ctx.arc(cx, cy, r, startRad, endRad)  -> degrees, clockwise
d = d.arcTo(cx, cy, r, startDeg, sweepDeg)                       # app.nim:222

# ctx.fillStyle = c; ctx.arc(cx, cy, r, 0, 2π)  -> the circle primitive
d = d.circle(bx, by, br * (1.0'f32 - f), rgba(255, 200, 130, 0.014))   # app.nim:219
```

**No ellipse primitive.** `circle` takes one radius. For `ctx.ellipse(x, y, rx, ry, …)`
the port builds SVG path data and lets Skia's path parser do the arc — this is the
single most reusable helper in the whole port (`app.nim:385-389`):

```nim
proc ellipsePath(x, y, rx, ry: float32): string =
  ## An <ellipse> as path data, so Draw.svgPath can fill it. Skia parses arcs.
  "M" & $(x - rx) & " " & $y &
  "a" & $rx & " " & $ry & " 0 1 0 " & $(rx * 2.0'f32) & " 0" &
  "a" & $rx & " " & $ry & " 0 1 0 " & $(-rx * 2.0'f32) & " 0Z"
```

It is then used exactly like any other path (`app.nim:474-476`):

```nim
d = d.svgPath(ellipsePath(cxp, 30, 78, 23), cx(0), by(0), U, 0xFFB3BABE'u32)
d = d.svgPath(ellipsePath(cxp, 29, 66, 17), cx(0), by(0), U, 0xFF231F1D'u32)
d = d.svgPath(ellipsePath(cxp, 29, 11,  4), cx(0), by(0), U, 0xFFC9CED1'u32)
```

That is how the 45 typewriter key caps get their chrome rim, cream cap and red shifted
legend from three concentric ellipses each (`app.nim:542-550`).

### Multiple sub-paths: `beginPath` → `moveTo`

Canvas starts a new sub-path on `beginPath()`. `Draw` has no such call — a `moveTo`
followed by a `lineTo` chain starts one, and a second `moveTo` starts another:

```nim
var wdeg = 0.0'f32
while wdeg < 360.0'f32:
  let px = cos(wdeg * 0.017453'f32) * 122.0'f32
  let py = sin(wdeg * 0.017453'f32) *  40.0'f32 - 8.0'f32
  if wdeg == 0: d = d.moveTo(px, py) else: d = d.lineTo(px, py)
  wdeg += 4.0'f32
d = d.close().stroke(rgba(122, 106, 74, 0.045), 1.0'f32).restore()   # app.nim:286-292
```

That `if wdeg == 0` guard is the idiom for "move on the first point, line after". Note
`0.017453` = degrees → radians, used inline because there is no constant worth
importing for a single multiply.

### One width per path

`stroke()` takes exactly one `width` (`paint.nim:55`) and applies it to the whole path
so far. A canvas path with two widths is two `Draw` values:

```nim
d = d.moveTo(px, cy(88.5)).lineTo(px, cy(101)).stroke(cRed, 1.6'f32 * U)   # app.nim:583
```

If the source drew sub-paths with different widths in one `beginPath()`, split them.

### Text

`d.text(x, y, size, argb, s, weight = 400)` (`paint.nim:63`). There is no font-family
parameter — `weight` is the only axis, and it is a raw number.

```nim
d = d.text(cx(486), by(111), 20.0'f32 * U, 0xFFE9EEF0'u32, "Marlowe", 400)   # app.nim:484
d = d.text(x, y + ty * U + dy + r * 0.35'f32 * U, fs * U, 0xFF2B2724'u32, label, 700)  # app.nim:561
```

The string is escaped internally for the command separator (`paint.nim:64`:
`s.replace(";", "\\;")`), so a label may contain `;` but not be relied on for it.

#### ⚠ Where `y` actually lands — measured, and it is not the baseline

Canvas puts `y` on the **baseline** (`ctx.textBaseline = 'alphabetic'` is the
default). Yantra does not. `Draw.text` ends up in `yk_text_draw`, which calls
`p->paint(canvas, x, y)` on an `SkParagraph`
(`libs/nimskia/src/skia_shim.cpp:291`) — so **`y` is the top of the paragraph
line box**. The doc comment on the native side says "Draws `s` with its top-left
at (x, y)" (`libs/nimskia/src/nimskia.nim:76`), and that comment is **wrong**; it
is the direct cause of mis-positioned text across the ports.

Offsets from the `y` you pass, in units of font size `S`:

| feature | lands at |
|---|---|
| cap / ascender ink top | `y + 0.375·S` |
| x-height top | `y + 0.583·S` |
| **baseline** | **`y + 1.0833·S`** |
| descender bottom | `y + 1.333·S` |

**MEASURED at S = 12, 24 and 48 with two independent strings — not documented.**
Empirical for the **default typeface and weight only**; a port that sets a
family or a non-default weight must re-probe before trusting these ratios.

The conversion every port needs:

```nim
proc yantraY(baselineY, size: float32): float32 = baselineY - 1.0833'f32 * size
```

and for `ctx.textBaseline = 'top'` (ink top at `Y`): `yantra_y = Y - 0.375 * size`.

That is exactly where the `r * 0.35'f32 * U` fudge on `app.nim:561` above comes
from — it compensates, by eye, for an origin the SDK never documented. Now that
the ratio is known, write `yantraY(...)` and drop the fudge.

**There is also no text measurement in the SDK.** `Draw.text` returns `Draw` and
nothing else (`paint.nim:63`) and has no `align` parameter; `measureText` exists
only on the native side (`libs/nimskia/src/nimskia.nim:68`, `:122`). Centring,
right-alignment and wrapping all have to be estimated.

## 2. Transforms

| Canvas | Yantra | Note |
|---|---|---|
| `ctx.translate(x, y)` | `d.translate(x, y)` | value-returning: `d = d.translate(...)` |
| `ctx.scale(x, y)` | `d.scale(sx, sy)` | `sy` defaults to `0'f32` (`paint.nim:78`), and the shim reads `y == 0` as "uniform" (`skia_shim.cpp:1410`: `cv->scale(x, y == 0 ? x : y)`) — so `d.scale(2)` *is* `d.scale(2, 2)`. Passing both is still clearer. |
| `ctx.rotate(rad)` | `d.rotate(degrees)` | **degrees**, and the arc angles are degrees too, so radians appear only in `sin`/`cos` |
| `ctx.save()` / `ctx.restore()` | `d.save()` / `d.restore()` | also value-returning |

```nim
dd = dd.scale(2.0'f32, 2.0'f32).translate(px, py).rotate(rot * 57.29578'f32)  # app.nim:334
```

`rot * 57.29578'f32` is radians → degrees, inlined. `app.nim:334` draws one glyph plate;
`app.nim:237` scales the whole paper layer 2× (`d = d.scale(2.0'f32, 2.0'f32)`), which
is how the port matches the source's `devicePixelRatio`-sized canvas without a
`devicePixelRatio` concept existing anywhere in Yantra.

## 3. Clipping

```nim
d = d.clip(x, y, w, h)     # paint.nim:81 — save()/restore() it like any transform
```

Used sparingly in the port. When a clipping region is static, the cleaner Yantra
answer is a `Layer` sized to the region and a `d.layer(l, x, y)` blit, because the
layer *is* already cropped.

## 4. Alpha: `globalAlpha` and `rgba(...)`

Three tools, all real:

```nim
d = d.alpha(0.45)                                   # scoped, paint.nim:80
d = d.rect(x, y, w, h, withA(cEnamel, 0.45))        # per-command, via the colour
d = d.circle(x, y, r, rgba(255, 200, 130, 0.014))   # a helper that builds ARGB
```

The port has one helper for the second, which is the cheapest way to keep alpha out of
the command string:

```nim
proc withA(c: uint32; a: float32): uint32 =                    # app.nim:381-383
  (c and 0x00FFFFFF'u32) or
    (uint32(clamp(int32(a * 255.0'f32 + 0.5'f32), 0'i32, 255'i32)) shl 24)
```

and one for the third, used for every `rgba(r, g, b, a)` in the source:

```nim
proc rgba(r, g, b: int32; a: float32): uint32 =                # app.nim:67-70
  proc q(v: int32): int32 = clamp(v, 0'i32, 255'i32)
  result = (uint32(q(int32(a * 255.0'f32 + 0.5'f32))) shl 24) or
           (uint32(q(r)) shl 16) or (uint32(q(g)) shl 8) or uint32(q(b))
```

Note the nested `proc q` inside `rgba`: Nim allows it, it is evaluated per call, and it
keeps the clamping in one place. `clamp` comes from `import std/math` (`app.nim:16`).

**Alpha 0 is a real 0 in `Draw`** (unlike node colours, where `style.nim:23-27` clamps
alpha to 1..255 so that a transparent colour stays distinguishable from an opaque
`0x000000`). `d.rect(0, 0, SW, SH, 0x00000000'u32)` at `app.nim:661` is a genuine no-op
fill, used to punch the page background through.

## 5. Composite operations

`d.blend(mode)` (`paint.nim:73`) takes any of the 29 Skia modes declared at
`paint.nim:14-17` — `bClear, bSrc, bDst, bSrcOver, bDstOver, bSrcIn, bDstIn, bSrcOut,
bDstOut, bSrcATop, bDstATop, bXor, bPlus, bModulate, bScreen, bOverlay, bDarken,
bLighten, bColorDodge, bColorBurn, bHardLight, bSoftLight, bDifference, bExclusion,
bMultiply, bHue, bSaturation, bColor, bLuminosity`.

| `ctx.globalCompositeOperation` | Yantra |
|---|---|
| `'multiply'` | `d.blend(bMultiply)` |
| `'screen'` | `d.blend(bScreen)` |
| `'overlay'` | `d.blend(bOverlay)` |
| `'difference'` | `d.blend(bDifference)` |
| `'destination-out'` | `d.blend(eraser)` — `const eraser* = bDstOut` (`paint.nim:21`) |
| `'source-over'` (the default) | nothing to write |
| `'lighter'` / `'plus-lighter'` | `d.blend(bPlus)` — see below for the premultiplied case |

`blend` is also how a `Layer` is cleared — see §9.

### The `bPlus` / `plus-lighter` case — CLOSED in source

**What the blend "lambda" actually is.** There is no lambda and no per-op colour maths.
`Draw.blend` (`sdk/ui/paint.nim:73`) appends the literal bytes `"b <ordinal>;"` to the command
string, and `runCmds` reads it in one line —
`libs/nimskia/src/skia_shim.cpp:1366`:

```cpp
case 'b': { int m = (int)r.num(); if (m >= 0 && m <= (int)SkBlendMode::kLastMode) blend = (SkBlendMode)m; break; }
```

It stores the value in a local `SkBlendMode blend` (`skia_shim.cpp:1309`) that is stamped onto
the paint of every **subsequent** op (`skia_shim.cpp:1326` `paint.setBlendMode(blend)`), and it is
saved/restored around `save`/`restore` (`skia_shim.cpp:1364-1365`). There is no lookup table:
the SDK enum's ordinal **is** cast straight to `SkBlendMode`, which is why `sdk/ui/paint.nim:14`
declares `Blend` as "Skia's blend modes, in its order". `bClear = 0` … `bLuminosity = 28`, and
the bounds check rejects anything outside that.

**The premultiplied case.** Yantra does **no** premultiplication and **no** clamping of its own.
Colours reach the paint as straight, non-premultiplied 8-bit ARGB —
`skia_shim.cpp:1327`: `auto colour = [&](unsigned argb) { paint.setColor(SkColor(argb)); if (alpha < 1) paint.setAlphaf(paint.getAlphaf() * alpha); };`
— and the blend arithmetic is entirely Skia's. So:

- **Fully opaque source and destination: `bPlus` is exactly what `plus-lighter` does.** The
  `alpha < 1` branch is not taken, and additive compositing of opaque colours is the same
  operation in both. Use it without hesitation.
- **Source colour with alpha < 255: not bit-identical to the canvas spec.** Canvas2D defines
  `plus-lighter` on non-premultiplied components with the result alpha forced to 1, whereas
  Skia's `kPlus` operates inside Skia's premultiplied pipeline. I did **not** read Skia's header
  to restate the difference component by component — `third_party/skia-src-m143` contains no
  headers in this checkout (7 files, all under `src/`), so I cannot quote it. If your port
  accumulates translucent glows with `plus-lighter`, render one and compare; do not assume the
  two are interchangeable.

**One more difference worth knowing.** `b` applies from the next op onward and is *not* scoped to
a single command, unlike canvas, where `globalCompositeOperation` is a canvas-wide state you
reset. A `blend` you forget to undo colours every later stroke on that `Draw`.

## 6. `ctx.filter` → layer filters

There is **no per-command filter**. `ctx.filter = 'blur(4px)'` becomes: draw into a
`newLayer`, then filter the layer in place.

```nim
type
  Layer* = distinct int32                     # paint.nim:13
  Filter* = enum
    blur, brightness, contrast, saturation, invert, greyscale, sepia, fade   # paint.nim:18-19

proc newLayer*(w, h: int): Layer                       # paint.nim:102
proc draw*(l: Layer; d: Draw)                          # paint.nim:107
proc clear*(l: Layer)                                  # paint.nim:110
proc filter*(l: Layer; kind: Filter; amount = 1'f32)   # paint.nim:111
proc read*(l: Layer; x, y, w, h: int): seq[byte]       # paint.nim:114
proc write*(l: Layer; x, y, w, h: int; rgba: openArray[byte])   # paint.nim:120
proc free*(l: Layer)                                   # paint.nim:124
```

`amount` units, per the `filter` doc comment (`paint.nim:112-113`): **blur in px**,
brightness −1..1, contrast / saturation 1 = unchanged, fade 0..1.

```nim
paperLayer = newLayer(int(PW * 2.0), int(PH * 2.0))   # app.nim:928
paperLayer.filter(blur, 3.0'f32)                       # e.g. a soft shadow layer
```

Two important properties:

- `Layer.draw` is **one-shot and cumulative** (`paint.nim:108`): "Draws into the layer
  once; it stays there (strokes accumulate)." This is what makes a `Layer` the right
  home for anything static *and* the right home for anything that accumulates (a
  paint program, a simulation).
- `filter` is **in place** and destructive. Filter a throwaway layer, never the one you
  still need sharp.

## 7. Gradients — the one thing `Draw` cannot do

**`Draw` has no gradient primitive.** There is no `createLinearGradient`, no
`createRadialGradient`, no shader, no gradient stop list in `paint.nim`. This is the
single biggest gap in the Canvas2D mapping, and it is a *different* situation from a
CSS gradient on a node (see `CSS-SHADOWS-AND-EFFECTS.md` §1 — a node **does** have a
2-stop linear gradient, `panel(fill = linear(a, b, 90))`).

Two substitutions, both used in `app.nim`.

### 7a. Linear → a stack of flat bands

The source's 2-stop vertical gradient over a plank becomes 12 bands, each a flat fill
whose colour is interpolated by hand (`app.nim:138-152`):

```nim
# plank body: the source's 2-stop vertical gradient, as 12 bands
var band = 0
while band < 12:
  let f = band.float32 / 11.0'f32
  let cA = t[0] + 6
  let cB = t[0] - 8
  let cC = t[1] + 4
  let cD = t[1] - 6
  let cE = t[2] + 2
  let cF = t[2] - 4
  d = d.rect(0, y + ph * f, w, ph / 12.0'f32 + 1.0'f32,
             rgba(cA + int32((cB - cA).float32 * f),
                  cC + int32((cD - cC).float32 * f),
                  cE + int32((cF - cE).float32 * f), 1.0))
  inc band
```

The `+ 1.0'f32` on the band height is deliberate and load-bearing: bands are placed at
`f * ph` and are `ph/12` tall, so a 1 px overlap hides the seam that two-decimal
float serialisation (`paint.nim:23-26`) would otherwise open between them. **Every
banded fill in a port needs this overlap.**

For a *small* shape — a few hundred pixels tall — 3 bands are enough, and the port has a
helper that reproduces a 3-stop gradient by dropping the end stops in from above and
below the mid stop (`app.nim:391-397`):

```nim
proc gradShape(d: var Draw; path: string; x, y, sc: float32; top, mid, bot: uint32) =
  ## The source fills these paths with a real vertical linear gradient. Draw has
  ## no gradient primitive, so: the mid stop flat, then the top stop dropped in
  ## from above and the bottom stop from below — the same stops, same direction.
  d = d.svgPath(path, x, y, sc, mid)
  d = d.svgPath(path, x, y - sc * 7.0'f32, sc, withA(top, 0.45))
  d = d.svgPath(path, x, y + sc * 5.0'f32, sc, withA(bot, 0.40))
```

Used for the platen, the side cheek, the shell and the deck (`app.nim:411, 458-461`).
It is cheap, it is directionally correct, and it is *not* a gradient — at close range it
shows three bands. For a large area, go back to 12+ bands.

### 7b. Radial → concentric rings

`ctx.createRadialGradient` becomes a stack of shrinking circles, alpha falling off with
radius (`app.nim:198-211`):

```nim
# lamplight from the upper left — 26 concentric rings, additive
let lx = w * 0.18
let ly = -h * 0.12
let lr = sqrt(w * w + h * h) * 0.95'f32
var s = 0
while s < 26:
  let f = s.float32 / 25.0'f32
  let a = (1.0'f32 - f) * (if f < 0.37'f32: 0.36'f32
                 elif f < 0.78'f32: 0.10'f32 else: 0.30'f32)
  let c = if f < 0.37: rgba(255, 214, 150, a)
          elif f < 0.78: rgba(255, 190, 120, a)
          else: rgba(12, 5, 1, a)
  d = d.circle(lx, ly, lr * (1.0'f32 - f), c)
  inc s
```

Read it as: the light's core is 26 translucent discs of decreasing radius, each so
faint they stack into a falloff. The three alpha regimes (0.36 core, 0.10 mid,
0.30 warm rim) are a transcription of the source's stop list, and they are **data** —
change the numbers to change the light.

The same trick, cheaper, for a soft shadow (`app.nim:663-668`):

```nim
var e = 0
while e < 14:
  let f = e.float32 / 13.0'f32
  d = d.circle(MX + MW / 2.0, MY + 300.0'f32 * U, MW * 0.56 * (1.0'f32 - f * 0.4),
           rgba(12, 6, 2, 0.05))
  inc e
```

**Cost:** these loops run once, into a `Layer`. They are the reason the desk is
`deskLayer.draw(d)` (`app.nim:228`) and never rebuilt per frame. Never emit 26 rings
inside `buildScene()`.

## 8. PRNG transcription — make the port deterministic

A page whose art uses `Math.random()` will not port reproducibly: every run differs,
every screenshot diff is noise, and re-opening a saved document re-randomises it.
The fix is to transcribe the page's own generator, seed it from something stable, and
store the seed with the data.

The page's was `mulberry32`. Here it is, line for line (`app.nim:49-61`):

```nim
type Rng = object
  a: uint32

proc rngInit(seed: int32): Rng = Rng(a: uint32(seed) and 0x7FFFFFFF'u32)

proc nextF(r: var Rng): float32 =
  r.a = r.a + 0x6D2B79F5'u32
  var t = r.a
  t = (t xor (t shr 15)) * (1'u32 or t)
  t = t + ((t xor (t shr 7)) * (61'u32 or t)) xor t
  result = (t xor (t shr 14)).float32 / 4294967296.0'f32

proc irnd(r: var Rng; n: int32): int32 = int32(r.nextF() * n.float32)
```

Notes that matter when transcribing any PRNG:

- `0xFFFFFFFF'u32` and `4294967296.0'f32` are literal because there is no `2^32` and no
  `float64`. Every constant is spelled in the target width.
- `1'u32 or t` and `61'u32 or t` are JS's `|1` and `|0` written as Nim `or` — JS coerces
  to a 32-bit **signed** int, Nim's `or` on `uint32` is unsigned. The results agree for
  the low bit pattern this generator depends on; a different generator may not.
- **One seed per visual element, not one seed for the scene.** `Stroke` carries its own
  `seed` (`app.nim:73-77`), assigned at keystroke time
  (`app.nim:734: seed: (int32(nowMs) + seedCounter * 7919) and 0x7FFFFFFF`) and used to
  rebuild that one stamp (`app.nim:317: var r = rngInit(s.seed)`).

That last point is the reason a restored letter looks *the same*: `redrawAll()`
(`app.nim:342-344`) repaints the paper from the sheet number and re-stamps every stroke
from its own seed. Re-running `nextF` in a different order — or from one shared
generator — would give every letter a different set of imperfections on reload.

> **Nim trap: `for x in seq` iterates copies.** `redrawAll` works because
> `for st in strokes: stampOne(st)` only *reads*. Anything that *writes* must index:
> `for i in 0 ..< motes.len: motes[i].y = motes[i].y - 0.00064'f32 * motes[i].s`
> (`app.nim:643`). Writing `for m in motes: m.y -= …` gives
> `Error: type mismatch … expression 'm.y' is immutable, not 'var'`.

If the source page used `Math.random()` directly rather than a named PRNG, seed a
single generator once per paint pass and **capture the outputs into your own data** at
port time, or accept that the art differs per run and say so.

## 9. Layers: paint once, blit per frame

The mental model that made the typewriter tractable:

| Page concept | Yantra |
|---|---|
| a static background that never changes | `newLayer` + `paintDesk()` once, then `d.layer(deskLayer, 0, 0)` every frame (`app.nim:660`) |
| a canvas you keep painting into (a document, a paint app) | a `Layer` you `draw` into repeatedly — strokes accumulate |
| a region that must be moved as a unit (the sheet in the platen) | a `Layer`, blitted with an offset and scale (`app.nim:617`) |
| `ctx.clearRect` on a layer | `layer.clear()` — implemented as `blend(bClear)` + a huge rect (`paint.nim:110`) |

```nim
var deskLayer: Layer            # app.nim:114
var paperLayer: Layer            # app.nim:115

deskLayer.clear()                # app.nim:227
deskLayer.draw(d)                # app.nim:228
```

`d.layer(l, x, y, w = 0, h = 0)` (`paint.nim:67`) draws the layer at its own size
unless `w`/`h` are given. The paper is blitted scaled down to fit the machine
(`app.nim:617`):

```nim
d = d.layer(paperLayer, x0, paperY, PW * S, PH * S)
```

**Per-frame cost budget.** `buildScene()` (`app.nim:658-688`) assembles the whole
scene as one string every frame. Its cost is proportional to the *command count*, not
to the pixels of anything cached in a layer. Moving expensive work into a
`Layer` — the desk (`app.nim:122-228`), the paper (`app.nim:231-306`) — is the
difference between a scene that runs and one that does not.

## 10. `drawImage` and raw pixels

| Canvas | Yantra |
|---|---|
| `ctx.drawImage(img, x, y, w, h)` | `d.image(x, y, w, h, "file.png")` (`paint.nim:65`) |
| `ctx.drawImage(offscreenCanvas, …)` | `d.layer(l, x, y, w, h)` (`paint.nim:67`) |
| `ctx.getImageData` | `layer.read(x, y, w, h): seq[byte]` RGBA (`paint.nim:114`) |
| `ctx.putImageData` | `layer.write(x, y, w, h, rgba)` (`paint.nim:120`), or `d.pixels(buf, pw, ph, x, y, w, h)` (`paint.nim:70`) |

There is no filesystem: the app never names a path it does not declare, and the host
resolves assets from the app's own folder (`reference/17-pitfalls.md`, the
`@pstd@ssyncio.nim.c` row). `layer.read` returning a short buffer sets `result.len` to
0 (`paint.nim:119`) — treat an empty seq as "nothing there", never index it.

## 11. The traps, collected

Everything here is in `SKILL.md` §"Nim traps" with the measured error text. The three
that specifically bite Canvas2D ports:

1. **`d = d.something(...)`, never `d.something(...)`.** The bare form is not a
   compile error — it compiles and draws nothing. This is the one that costs an hour.
2. **`round` from `std/math` builds fine and faults at run time.** mini-libc's `round`
   is recursive, so a per-pixel loop overflows the stack. Use the port's `rnd32`
   (`app.nim:64`): `int32(if x < 0: x - 0.5 else: x + 0.5)`.
3. **`float64` compiles and is silently wrong on wasm32.** `1.0` in a `float32`
   expression is fine; a stray `float64` in a pixel loop produces correct-looking
   output that is wrong on a large fraction of inputs
   (`reference/16` §3.3). Write `1.0'f32` in art maths, as `app.nim` does everywhere.

================================================================
FILE: sdk/yantra.nim
================================================================
## Yantra SDK — the surface an app author writes.
##
## AN APP IS A WASM MODULE. It has no window, no filesystem and no network. It
## builds a description of a UI by calling the host, and the host decides what to
## do with it. That is the whole security model: the sandbox cannot reach the
## machine, it can only ask.
##
## HOW A CALL CROSSES THE BOUNDARY
##
##     your Nim code            wasm                  the engine (native)
##     -------------            ----                  -------------------
##     text(parent, "Hi")  ->   node_create(i32)   -> creates a node
##                              node_set_str(...)  -> reads your text out of
##                                                    wasm linear memory
##     onClick = proc()    ->   node_on(...)       -> engine remembers handler N
##
##     engine sees a click ->   yantra_dispatch(0) -> calls handler 0 in wasm
##
## STRINGS: a Nim string lives in wasm linear memory. We hand the engine a
## (pointer, length) pair and it copies the bytes out immediately. Nothing is
## retained on the wasm side, so there is no ownership puzzle.
##
## 3D: `scene3d` gives you a 3D viewport, and `model`/`camera`/`light` populate
## it. A scene with no camera or light gets sensible ones from the renderer, so
## the smallest working 3D app is two lines.

import std/options
import core/wire except Align, Easing; export NodeKind, Prop, Event

type
  Align* {.size: sizeof(int32).} = enum
    ## Text alignment. This was a DEAD property for a while: `pAlign` existed in
    ## the vocabulary and the renderer never emitted it, so every value an app
    ## set was silently discarded.
    alLeft   = 0
    alCenter = 1
    alRight  = 2

import core/runtime, core/signals, core/persist, core/net, core/data, core/fmt, ui/widgets, ui/dsl, ui/control, ui/style, ui/theme, ui/icons, ui/motion, ui/units, ui/machine, ui/rich, ui/keys, ui/vlist, ui/sheet, ui/router, ui/blocks, ui/scene3d, ui/input, ui/paint, ui/physics, ui/character
export runtime, signals, persist, net, data, fmt, widgets, dsl, control, style, theme, icons, motion, units, machine, rich, keys, vlist, sheet, router, blocks, scene3d, input, paint, physics, character
## NOTE (sdk-guardian 2026-11-05): core/tokens is INTENTIONALLY not imported/
## exported here. Tokens `px`/`ms`/`s` (plain int wire values) collide with
## ui/units `px`/`ms`/`s` templates (Px/Ms distinct types) and motion/timeline
## `ms`/`s` (Duration) — exporting both makes `16.px` / `200.ms` ambiguous and
## breaks every app. Use `import core/tokens` explicitly in the module that
## needs it. core/data (`fetchSales`, `TimeRange`, …) and core/fmt (`fmt`,
## `fmtFixed`, …) grep zero clashes in sdk/ + apps/, so they are prelude-safe.

proc fontSize*(node: Node, value: int32)
proc x*(node: Node, value: float32)
proc y*(node: Node, value: float32)
proc visible*(node: Node, value: bool)
proc opacity*(node: Node, value: float32)
proc color*(node: Node, r, g, b: int32)
proc source*(node: Node, value: string)
proc scale*(node: Node, value: float32)
proc rotation*(node: Node, rx, ry, rz: float32)
proc position*(node: Node, px, py, pz: float32)
proc metalness*(node: Node, value: float32)
proc roughness*(node: Node, value: float32)
proc spacing*(node: Node, value: int32)
proc padding*(node: Node, all: int32)
proc radius*(node: Node, value: int32)
proc border*(node: Node, width: int32, r, g, b: int32)
proc align*(node: Node, a: Align)

proc scrollX*(node: Node, v: int32) = node.set(pScrollX, v)
proc scrollY*(node: Node, v: int32) = node.set(pScrollY, v)

# --- tabs setters ----------------------------------------------------------

proc tabIndex*(node: Node, v: int32) = node.set(pTabIndex, v)

# --- table/list setters ----------------------------------------------------

proc columns*(node: Node, v: string) = node.set(pColumns, v)
proc rows*(node: Node, v: string) = node.set(pRows, v)
proc selection*(node: Node, v: string) = node.set(pSelection, v)
proc sortable*(node: Node, v: bool) = node.set(pSortable, int32(if v: 1 else: 0))
proc filterable*(node: Node, v: bool) = node.set(pFilterable, int32(if v: 1 else: 0))

# --- media setters ---------------------------------------------------------

proc src*(node: Node, v: string) = node.set(pSrc, v)
proc poster*(node: Node, v: string) = node.set(pPoster, v)
proc autoplay*(node: Node, v: bool) = node.set(pAutoplay, int32(if v: 1 else: 0))
proc loop*(node: Node, v: bool) = node.set(pLoop, int32(if v: 1 else: 0))
proc muted*(node: Node, v: bool) = node.set(pMuted, int32(if v: 1 else: 0))
proc volume*(node: Node, v: float32) = node.set(pVolume, v)
proc playbackRate*(node: Node, v: float32) = node.set(pPlaybackRate, v)

# --- canvas setters --------------------------------------------------------

proc canvasSize*(node: Node, w, h: int32) =
  node.width(w)
  node.height(h)
  node.set(pCanvasWidth, w)
  node.set(pCanvasHeight, h)

# --- overlay setters -------------------------------------------------------

proc open*(node: Node, v: bool) = node.set(pModalOpen, int32(if v: 1 else: 0))
proc title*(node: Node, v: string) = node.set(pModalTitle, v)
proc closeOnEsc*(node: Node, v: bool) = node.set(pCloseOnEsc, int32(if v: 1 else: 0))
proc closeOnClickOutside*(node: Node, v: bool) = node.set(pCloseOnClickOutside, int32(if v: 1 else: 0))

# --- event handlers --------------------------------------------------------

proc on*(node: Node, event: Event, handler: proc()) =
  node.addHandler(event, handler)

proc onInput*(node: Node, handler: proc()) = node.on(evInput, handler)

proc trackScroll*(node: Node; y: Signal[float32]) =
  ## Keeps `y` at the node's scroll offset (px): the engine sends Scroll when it changes.
  node.on(evScroll, proc () = batch(proc () = y.set(yNodeSize(int32(node.id), 2))))

proc lazyFirst*(y, rowH: float32; overscan = 4): int =
  ## The first row a lazy list builds when scrolled to `y` (a few above what shows).
  max(0, int(y / max(rowH, 1)) - overscan)

proc lazyLast*(y, h, rowH: float32; n: int; overscan = 4): int =
  ## One past the last row it builds: what shows in `h`, and a few below; never past `n`.
  min(n, int((y + h) / max(rowH, 1)) + 1 + overscan)

proc trackSize*(node: Node; w, h: Signal[float32]) =
  ## Keeps `w` and `h` at the node's laid-out size (px): the engine sends Resize when it
  ## changes, before painting. `sized w, h:` in the DSL uses it [S-LAY-2].
  node.on(evResize, proc () = batch(proc () =
    w.set(yNodeSize(int32(node.id), 0))
    h.set(yNodeSize(int32(node.id), 1))))
proc onFocus*(node: Node, handler: proc()) = node.on(evFocus, handler)
proc onBlur*(node: Node, handler: proc()) = node.on(evBlur, handler)
proc onScroll*(node: Node, handler: proc()) = node.on(evScroll, handler)
proc onTabChange*(node: Node, handler: proc()) = node.on(evTabChange, handler)
proc onSelectionChange*(node: Node, handler: proc()) = node.on(evSelectionChange, handler)
proc onMediaEnd*(node: Node, handler: proc()) = node.on(evMediaEnd, handler)
proc onMediaError*(node: Node, handler: proc()) = node.on(evMediaError, handler)
proc onCanvasDraw*(node: Node, handler: proc()) = node.on(evCanvasDraw, handler)
proc onMediaReady*(node: Node, handler: proc()) = node.on(evMediaReady, handler)
proc onTimeUpdate*(node: Node, handler: proc()) = node.on(evTimeUpdate, handler)

proc mediaTime*(node: Node): float32 = yNodeSize(int32(node.id), 3)
  ## A video's or audio's position in seconds (as of the last TimeUpdate).
proc mediaDuration*(node: Node): float32 = yNodeSize(int32(node.id), 4)
  ## Its length in seconds (0 until MediaReady; 0 if the file doesn't say).
proc trackMedia*(node: Node; time, duration: Signal[float32]) =
  ## Keeps `time` and `duration` at the node's playing position and length (P3).
  let update = proc () = batch(proc () =
    time.set(yNodeSize(int32(node.id), 3))
    duration.set(yNodeSize(int32(node.id), 4)))
  node.on(evTimeUpdate, update)
  node.on(evMediaReady, update)
proc waveform*(node: Node): seq[float32] =
  ## An audio node's peaks (0..1), as many as its `waveform` prop asked for, once MediaReady
  ## has come: a voice note's bars.
  let n = int(yNodeSize(int32(node.id), 5))
  for i in 0 ..< n: result.add yNodeSize(int32(node.id), int32(1000 + i))

# --- animations ------------------------------------------------------------

proc animate*(node: Node; target: string; to: float32; duration: int32 = 300;
              easing: Easing = easeOutCubic; startVal: Option[float32] = none(float32);
              delay: int32 = 0; loop: int32 = 1; yoyo: bool = false;
              trigger: string = "mount") =
  ## Declarative animation. The engine translates this to a Qt Quick animation.
  ##
  ## target: property to animate ("opacity", "x", "y", "width", "height", "scale", "rotation")
  ## to: end value
  ## duration: milliseconds
  ## easing: easing curve
  ## startVal: start value (optional, defaults to current)
  ## delay: delay before start (ms)
  ## loop: number of loops (0 = infinite)
  ## yoyo: reverse on each loop
  ## trigger: "mount" | "click" | "hover" | "focus" | "prop:<name>"
  node.set(pAnimTarget, target)
  node.set(pAnimTo, to)
  node.set(pAnimDuration, duration)
  node.set(pAnimEasing, int32(easing))
  node.set(pAnimDelay, delay)
  node.set(pAnimLoop, loop)
  node.set(pAnimYoyo, int32(if yoyo: 1 else: 0))
  node.set(pAnimTrigger, trigger)
  if startVal.isSome: node.set(pAnimFrom, startVal.get)

proc transition*(node: Node; kind: string; duration: int32 = 300; easing: Easing = easeOutCubic) =
  ## Transition animation for enter/exit/move/resize/opacity.
  ## kind: "enter" | "exit" | "move" | "resize" | "opacity"
  node.set(pTransition, kind)
  node.set(pTransDuration, duration)
  node.set(pTransEasing, int32(easing))



================================================================
FILE: sdk/ui/dsl.nim
